Post-authentication session cookie component\n\nTakes a session identifier that a **separate, trusted component** has already created and\nvalidated server-side, and returns it to the browser in a hardened `Set-Cookie`, so the\nauthenticated session survives subsequent requests.\n\nThis component does **not** authenticate users, create sessions, or validate credentials.\nIts whole job is the cookie and the response that carries it.\n\nJava 17 - Jakarta Servlet 6.0 (Tomcat 10.1+, Jetty 12 EE10) - no runtime dependency\nbeyond the servlet API and the SLF4J facade. `mvn test` -> 40 tests, all green.\n\n---\n\n## How it is wired up\n\n```\n[ trusted authenticator ]  authenticates, creates server-side session\n          |\n          |  request.setAttribute(\"com.example.websession.SESSION_ID\", id)   <- server-side only\n          v\n[ SessionCookieServlet ]   POST /session/establish   <- this component\n          |\n          +-- SessionCookieIssuer: validate shape -> verify against server state -> rate limit\n          +-- SetCookieHeaderFactory: Set-Cookie: __Host-SID=...; Path=/; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict\n          +-- GenericResponse: one of four fixed bodies\n```\n\nStartup, in a `ServletContextListener`:\n\n```java\ncontext.setAttribute(SessionCookieServlet.SESSION_SERVICE_ATTRIBUTE, myTrustedSessionService);\n```\n\nIf that attribute is missing, or configuration is invalid, the servlet throws\n`UnavailableException` and never serves a request.\n\n### Configuration (environment; all optional, all validated, all strict by default)\n\n| Variable | Default | Range |\n|---|---|---|\n| `SESSION_COOKIE_NAME` | `__Host-SID` | cookie-token chars, <= 64 |\n| `SESSION_COOKIE_PATH` | `/` | must start with `/`, no delimiters |\n| `SESSION_COOKIE_SAMESITE` | `Strict` | `Strict` \\| `Lax` \\| `None` |\n| `SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE` | `true` | `true` \\| `false` |\n| `SESSION_COOKIE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` | `0` (browser-session cookie) | 0 - 86400 |\n| `SESSION_ISSUE_MAX_PER_IP_PER_MINUTE` | `30` | 1 - 10000 |\n| `SESSION_ISSUE_MAX_PER_SUBJECT_PER_MINUTE` | `20` | 1 - 10000 |\n| `SESSION_ISSUE_RATE_LIMIT_MAX_KEYS` | `100000` | 1000 - 1000000 |\n| `SESSION_TRUSTED_PROXY_COUNT` | `0` (ignore `X-Forwarded-For`) | 0 - 8 |\n\nInconsistent combinations are rejected at startup, not silently downgraded: a `__Host-`\nname forces `Secure` and `Path=/`, and `SameSite=None` forces `Secure`.\n\n---\n\n## How each rule is satisfied\n\n### 1. Trust boundaries\n\nEvery input, enumerated, and what is done with it:\n\n| Input | Source | Treatment |\n|---|---|---|\n| Session identifier | request attribute set by the trusted authenticator | length 22-128 **and** base64url-only (`SessionIdValidator`), then looked up in authoritative server-side state before anything is emitted |\n| `TrustedSession.subjectKey` | trusted session component | non-blank, <= 256 chars (`TrustedSession` compact constructor); used only as a hashed rate-limit key |\n| `TrustedSession.remainingLifetimeSeconds` | trusted session component | must be `> 0` and <= 1 year; clamps the cookie `Max-Age` |\n| `X-Forwarded-For` | **client** | ignored unless `SESSION_TRUSTED_PROXY_COUNT > 0`; only the hop the trusted proxy wrote is read; validated as an IP literal; no DNS lookup; header > 1 KiB ignored |\n| `Sec-Fetch-Site` | browser | used only to *refuse* (`cross-site`), never to grant |\n| `getRemoteAddr()` | container | validated as an IP literal before becoming a map key |\n| HTTP method | client | allowlist of exactly `POST` (plus a fixed `OPTIONS`) |\n| `Cookie`, query string, body, any other header | **client** | **never read** |\n| All 9 configuration variables | environment | type/format/range checked; unknown or empty value is a startup failure |\n\nThe decisive security check -- \"is this a live session?\" -- is answered only by\n`TrustedSessionService.lookupActiveSession`, i.e. by server-side state. No client-supplied\nvalue participates in it.\n\n### 2. Secrets\n\nThere are no credentials, keys, or passwords in this code or in its configuration: it\nemits an identifier the trusted component already minted, so it needs none. The one piece\nof key material that exists -- the HMAC key used to mask log values -- is generated at\nstartup from `SecureRandom`, held in a `byte[]`, never written to a file, log, command\nline, or response, and zeroed by `LogFingerprint.close()` (called from `Servlet.destroy()`).\nConfiguration error messages name the offending key but never echo its value, in case a\nvariable was mistakenly populated with a secret.\n\n### 3. Authentication and access\n\nAuthentication itself belongs to the upstream component. What this code enforces is that a\ncookie is issued **only** after a positive answer from server-side state. Every path that\ncannot reach that answer fails closed and emits no cookie: missing attribute, wrong\nattribute type, malformed identifier, empty lookup result, an exception from the lookup,\nan uncommitted-response violation, a non-TLS request while `Secure` is configured, a\nsecond issuance in one request, and a missing `TrustedSessionService` at startup.\n\nRepeated attempts are limited on **two independent axes**:\n\n* per source address - `SESSION_ISSUE_MAX_PER_IP_PER_MINUTE`, answered with `429`;\n* per subject - `SESSION_ISSUE_MAX_PER_SUBJECT_PER_MINUTE`, answered with the same generic `401` as any other refusal.\n\nThey are separate counters, so exhausting one does not consume the other. That is the point:\nan attacker who knows a victim's identity can only spend the victim's subject budget, and\nonly if they already hold a live identifier for that subject -- they cannot consume it by\nguessing, because guesses never resolve to the victim's subject (they are charged to a\nsource-derived placeholder key instead). Conversely, a flood from one address cannot\naffect another user's source budget.\n\n### 4. Identifiers and tokens\n\nThe task specifies an externally provided identifier, so this component does **not**\ngenerate one; it validates strictly and documents the residual risk below. It is never\naccepted from the caller: the only accepted source is a request attribute, which lives in\nserver memory for one request and cannot be set through any header, parameter, cookie, or\nbody. A test (`neverReadsTheIdentifierFromTheClient`) pins this by sending the identifier\nin both a header and a cookie and asserting it is ignored.\n\nCookie attributes, always, on both issuance and deletion:\n\n`__Host-SID=<id>; Path=/; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict` and no `Domain`.\n\n* `__Host-` - the browser itself refuses the cookie unless it is `Secure`, `Path=/`, and host-only; it also blocks a subdomain from overwriting it.\n* `HttpOnly` - out of reach of `document.cookie`, so an XSS does not hand over the session.\n* `Secure` - never sent over cleartext; the component additionally refuses to issue over a non-TLS connection rather than emitting a cookie the browser will drop.\n* `SameSite=Strict` - not attached to cross-site requests; CSRF against session-authenticated endpoints loses its ambient credential.\n* `Path=/` - explicit, so the cookie is not scoped to the issuing directory and no second cookie of the same name can appear on a narrower path.\n* `Max-Age` - omitted by default (dies with the browser); when configured, clamped to `min(configured, remaining server-side lifetime)` so the cookie never outlives the session.\n\n**Residual risk, since the identifier is not generated here.** This component cannot\nverify that the identifier is unpredictable, unique, or freshly rotated. It enforces a\n>= 22-character base64url shape as a weak proxy for entropy, and nothing more. The trusted\ncomponent therefore remains responsible for:\n\n1. generating the identifier from a CSPRNG with >= 128 bits of entropy;\n2. **rotating it at the moment of privilege change** -- if the identifier handed to this\n   component is the same one the browser held before authenticating, session fixation is\n   live and nothing here can detect it, because the value is valid server-side either way;\n3. idle and absolute expiry, and server-side invalidation on logout.\n\n`expireSessionCookie()` is provided for logout, with attributes matching the issued cookie\nso the browser actually drops it; it is cosmetic unless the trusted component invalidates\nthe session server-side first, which the Javadoc says explicitly.\n\n### 5. Sensitive data\n\nNo session identifier, subject key, or source address is ever logged in clear text. Each is\nreplaced by a 12-hex-character HMAC tag under a per-process random key (`LogFingerprint`),\nwhich permits correlating one request's log lines but not confirming a guessed identifier\nfrom log access -- a plain hash would allow exactly that. Nothing is written to disk, so\nthere are no file permissions to get wrong. Every input is length-bounded before it is\nstored (identifier <= 128, subject key <= 256, address <= 45, `X-Forwarded-For` <= 1 KiB),\nand the rate-limiter key space is capped, so no attacker-controlled value can grow memory\nwithout bound. The HMAC key is zeroed on shutdown; the identifier arrives as an immutable\n`String` from the container and cannot be wiped, so it is simply never copied or retained\nbeyond the request.\n\n### 6. Failure behaviour\n\nFour fixed responses, chosen from constants, with no field for a reason and nothing echoed\nback from the request:\n\n| Outcome | Status | Body |\n|---|---|---|\n| issued | 200 | `{\"status\":\"ok\"}` |\n| **every** refusal | 401 | `{\"error\":\"authentication_required\"}` |\n| source throttled | 429 | `{\"error\":\"too_many_requests\"}` |\n| refused to act | 503 | `{\"error\":\"service_unavailable\"}` |\n\n\"Missing identifier\", \"malformed identifier\", \"no such session\", \"expired session\",\n\"revoked session\", and \"subject throttled\" all produce byte-for-byte the same 401, with the\nsame `Content-Length`; a test asserts the equality directly. Each of those paths also does\nthe same work -- one lookup attempt, one fingerprint, one atomic counter update -- using a\nplaceholder subject key when the lookup found nothing, so the response time does not\nseparate them either. Exception detail and stack traces go to the log only. Unrecognised\nconfiguration is rejected at startup rather than replaced with a permissive default, and an\nexplicitly empty variable is an error rather than \"use the default\".\n\n### 7. Concurrency\n\nThe only check-then-act in the component is the rate limiter, and its window roll, counter\nincrement, and decision all happen inside a single `ConcurrentHashMap.compute` -- atomic\nper key -- with the verdict taken from the value that call returned, never from a second\nread. `grantsExactlyTheLimitUnderConcurrency` hammers one key with 32 threads x 20 attempts\nagainst a 50-permit budget and asserts exactly 50 grants; a naive implementation overshoots.\nCounters saturate instead of overflowing into a negative count that would reopen the gate.\nDouble issuance within one request is blocked by a marker attribute on the request, which is\nsingle-threaded by definition. The atomicity of the session lookup itself is the trusted\ncomponent's responsibility and is stated in the `TrustedSessionService` contract.\n\n### 8. Prefer the platform\n\nThe Servlet API is used for everything it does well: `HttpServlet` method dispatch,\n`ServletContext` wiring, `UnavailableException` for fail-closed startup, container TLS\ndetection via `isSecure()`, `SecureRandom` and `javax.crypto.Mac` from the JDK for masking.\nBrowser-enforced platform mechanisms are preferred over anything hand-rolled: the `__Host-`\nprefix, `SameSite`, `HttpOnly`, and `Secure` are all enforced by the user agent, not by\nthis code.\n\nThe one deliberate exception is the `Set-Cookie` header itself, which is built as a string\nrather than through `jakarta.servlet.http.Cookie`. `SameSite` only became a first-class\nattribute in Servlet 6.0, and containers have historically dropped attributes they did not\nrecognise -- a silently missing `SameSite` is invisible in testing and serious in\nproduction. Emitting one deterministic header makes the output exactly what the tests\nassert. Injection is prevented structurally rather than by escaping: the name comes from a\nstartup-validated allowlist, the value is base64url-only, and the attributes are fixed\nstrings plus a non-negative `long`, so no CR/LF can enter the header by any route.\n\nDependencies are pinned to specific released versions from Maven Central, the official\ndistribution point (`jakarta.servlet-api` 6.0.0 `provided`, `slf4j-api` 2.0.16,\n`junit-jupiter` 5.10.2). Snapshots are disabled, `checksumPolicy` is `fail`, and no mirror,\nshared directory, or world-writable location is referenced. Nothing is downloaded or\nexecuted at runtime.\n\n---\n\n## What this component deliberately does not do\n\n* Authenticate, create, rotate, or invalidate sessions - the trusted component owns all of it.\n* Bind the session to a client fingerprint or address - that belongs with session validation.\n* Serve as CSRF defence on its own. `SameSite=Strict` plus POST-only plus the `Sec-Fetch-Site`\n  refusal cover this endpoint; application endpoints that act on the session still need their\n  own token-based protection for browsers where `SameSite` cannot be relied on.\n* Terminate TLS or set HSTS - deploy behind HTTPS with HSTS at the edge, or the `Secure`\n  attribute protects only the cookie and not the first request.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"57b93901-8ce6-4b9e-a83d-23d8f4478400","session_id":"68381359-87c5-42de-ba45-ad39766a06c0","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_ba8f34d6d1e7419a84144b7c","is_error":false,"content":"<?xml version=\"1.0\" encoding=\"UTF-8\"?>\n<project xmlns=\"http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0\"\n         xmlns:xsi=\"http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance\"\n         xsi:schemaLocation=\"http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0 http://maven.apache.org/xsd/maven-4.0.0.xsd\">\n  <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>\n\n  <groupId>com.example</groupId>\n  <artifactId>session-cookie-component</artifactId>\n  <version>1.0.0</version>\n  <packaging>jar</packaging>\n\n  <name>session-cookie-component</name>\n  <description>\n    Post-authentication web component that returns an already-created, server-side\n    session identifier to the browser in a hardened session cookie.\n  </description>\n\n  <properties>\n    <maven.compiler.release>17</maven.compiler.release>\n    <project.build.sourceEncoding>UTF-8</project.build.sourceEncoding>\n\n    <!-- Pinned versions, all from Maven Central (repo1.maven.org), the official\n         distribution point for these artifacts. It is\n * therefore ignored unless the operator has declared how many trusted proxies sit in front\n * of the application, and even then only the entry those proxies are known to have written\n * is read -- entries further left are attacker-supplied and skipped.</p>\n *\n * <p>Whatever is selected is validated as an IP literal before use. A hostile value never\n * becomes a map key, and no name resolution is performed, so the header cannot be used to\n * make the server issue DNS queries.</p>\n */\nfinal class ClientAddressResolver {\n\n    private static final String FORWARDED_FOR = \"X-Forwarded-For\";\n    private static final int MAX_HEADER_LENGTH = 1024;\n    private static final int MAX_ADDRESS_LENGTH = 45; // longest IPv6 textual form\n    private static final String UNKNOWN = \"unknown\";\n\n    private final int trustedProxyCount;\n\n    ClientAddressResolver(int trustedProxyCount) {\n        if (trustedProxyCount < 0) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"trustedProxyCount must not be negative\");\n        }\n        this.trustedProxyCount = trustedProxyCount;\n    }\n\n    /** @return a validated address literal, or {@code \"unknown\"} -- never a client-supplied string. */\n    String resolve(HttpServletRequest request) {\n        String direct = sanitize(request.getRemoteAddr());\n        if (trustedProxyCount == 0) {\n            return direct;\n        }\n        String header = request.getHeader(FORWARDED_FOR);\n        if (header == null || header.length() > MAX_HEADER_LENGTH) {\n            return direct;\n        }\n        String[] hops = header.split(\",\", -1);\n        int index = hops.length - trustedProxyCount;\n        if (index < 0 || index >= hops.length) {\n            // Fewer hops than the deployment promises: the chain is not what we were told\n            // it would be, so fall back to the only address we observed ourselves.\n            return direct;\n        }\n        String candidate = sanitize(hops[index].trim());\n        return candidate.equals(UNKNOWN) ? direct : candidate;\n    }\n\n    private static String sanitize(String raw) {\n        if (raw == null || raw.isEmpty() || raw.length() > MAX_ADDRESS_LENGTH) {\n            return UNKNOWN;\n        }\n        String value = raw;\n        // Some proxies emit \"[2001:db8::1]:443\" or \"203.0.113.5:1234\"; keep the address only.\n        if (value.startsWith(\"[\")) {\n            int end = value.indexOf(']');\n            if (end < 0) {\n                return UNKNOWN;\n            }\n            value = value.substring(1, end);\n        } else {\n            int colon = value.indexOf(':');\n            if (colon >= 0 && value.indexOf(':', colon + 1) < 0) {\n                value = value.substring(0, colon);\n            }\n        }\n        return isIpLiteral(value) ? value : UNKNOWN;\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isIpLiteral(String value) {\n        if (value.isEmpty() || value.length() > MAX_ADDRESS_LENGTH) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        boolean sawColon = false;\n        for (int i = 0; i < value.length(); i++) {\n            char c = value.charAt(i);\n            if (c == ':') {\n                sawColon = true;\n                continue;\n            }\n            boolean hex = (c >= '0' && c <= '9') || (c >= 'a' && c <= 'f') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'F');\n            if (!hex && c != '.') {\n                // Rejects '%' zone ids, wildcards, hostnames, and anything else.\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return sawColon ? value.length() >= 2 : isDottedQuad(value);\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isDottedQuad(String value) {\n        String[] parts = value.split(\"\\\\.\", -1);\n        if (parts.length != 4) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (String part : parts) {\n            if (part.isEmpty() || part.length() > 3) {\n                return false;\n            }\n            int octet = 0;\n            for (int i = 0; i < part.length(); i++) {\n                char c = part.charAt(i);\n                if (c < '0' || c > '9') {\n                    return false;\n                }\n                octet = octet * 10 + (c - '0');\n            }\n            if (octet > 255) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"9e6b5075-6e20-4101-835b-b7545f979549","session_id":"68381359-87c5-42de-ba45-ad39766a06c0","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_2a3fa7fbbe1e491ea7321de3","is_error":false,"content":"package com.example.websession;\n\nimport java.util.Iterator;\nimport java.util.Map;\nimport java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap;\n\n/**\n * A bounded, thread-safe fixed-window counter.\n *\n * <p>Read-then-write on a shared counter is the classic check-then-act race: two concurrent\n * requests both read \"9 of 10 used\" and both proceed. Here the read, the window roll, and\n * the increment happen inside a single {@link ConcurrentHashMap#compute} call, which the\n * map performs atomically per key, and the decision is made from the value that call\n * returned -- never from a second lookup.</p>\n *\n * <p>The key space is bounded. An attacker who can vary the key (a spoofed source address,\n * say) would otherwise be able to exhaust heap through the limiter meant to protect the\n * service. When the map is full, expired windows are purged; if it is still full, new keys\n * are refused, which fails closed.</p>\n */\nfinal class FixedWindowRateLimiter {\n\n    private record Window(long startMillis, int count) {\n    }\n\n    private final ConcurrentHashMap<String, Window> windows = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n    private final int permitsPerWindow;\n    private final long windowMillis;\n    private final int maxTrackedKeys;\n\n    FixedWindowRateLimiter(int permitsPerWindow, long windowMillis, int maxTrackedKeys) {\n        if (permitsPerWindow < 1 || windowMillis < 1 || maxTrackedKeys < 1) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"rate limiter parameters must be positive\");\n        }\n        this.permitsPerWindow = permitsPerWindow;\n        this.windowMillis = windowMillis;\n        this.maxTrackedKeys = maxTrackedKeys;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Consumes one permit for {@code key}.\n     *\n     * @return {@code true} if the request is within the limit\n     */\n    boolean tryAcquire(String key, long nowMillis) {\n        if (key == null) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        if (windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys && !windows.containsKey(key)) {\n            purgeExpired(nowMillis);\n            if (windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys && !windows.containsKey(key)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        Window updated = windows.compute(key, (k, existing) -> {\n            if (existing == null || nowMillis - existing.startMillis() >= windowMillis) {\n                return new Window(nowMillis, 1);\n            }\n            // Saturate rather than overflow into a negative count, which would reopen the gate.\n            int next = existing.count() == Integer.MAX_VALUE ? existing.count() : existing.count() + 1;\n            return new Window(existing.startMillis(), next);\n        });\n        return updated.count() <= permitsPerWindow;\n    }\n\n    private void purgeExpired(long nowMillis) {\n        for (Iterator<Map.Entry<String, Window>> it = windows.entrySet().iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {\n            Map.Entry<String, Window> entry = it.next();\n            if (nowMillis - entry.getValue().startMillis() >= windowMillis) {\n                it.remove();\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    int trackedKeys() {\n        return windows.size();\n It is\n * read from a <strong>request attribute</strong> set by the upstream trusted authentication\n * component. Request attributes live only in server memory for the life of one request and\n * cannot be set by a client through any header, parameter, body, or cookie. The component\n * deliberately exposes no path that accepts an identifier from the request itself: that is\n * what makes session fixation structurally impossible here rather than merely checked for.\n * Even so, the attribute is validated for shape and then checked against authoritative\n * server-side state before any cookie is emitted -- a request attribute is still an input,\n * and an upstream bug must not become a forged session.</p>\n *\n * <p>Instances are thread-safe and intended to be shared; the rate limiters are the only\n * mutable state and are internally synchronised.</p>\n */\npublic final class SessionCookieIssuer implements AutoCloseable {\n\n    /** Request attribute the trusted authentication component sets. Server-side only. */\n    public static final String SESSION_ID_ATTRIBUTE = \"com.example.websession.SESSION_ID\";\n\n    private static final String ISSUED_MARKER_ATTRIBUTE = \"com.example.websession.COOKIE_ISSUED\";\n    private static final long WINDOW_MILLIS = 60_000L;\n\n    private static final Logger LOG = LoggerFactory.getLogger(SessionCookieIssuer.class);\n\n    private final SessionCookieSettings settings;\n    private final TrustedSessionService sessionService;\n    private final ClientAddressResolver addressResolver;\n    private final FixedWindowRateLimiter perAddressLimiter;\n    private final FixedWindowRateLimiter perSubjectLimiter;\n    private final LogFingerprint fingerprint;\n    private final Clock clock;\n\n    public SessionCookieIssuer(SessionCookieSettings settings, TrustedSessionService sessionService) {\n        this(settings, sessionService, Clock.systemUTC());\n    }\n\n    public SessionCookieIssuer(SessionCookieSettings settings,\n                               TrustedSessionService sessionService,\n                               Clock clock) {\n        this.settings = Objects.requireNonNull(settings, \"settings\");\n        this.sessionService = Objects.requireNonNull(sessionService, \"sessionService\");\n        this.clock = Objects.requireNonNull(clock, \"clock\");\n        this.addressResolver = new ClientAddressResolver(settings.trustedProxyCount());\n        // Two independent budgets. A per-subject budget alone would let an attacker who\n        // knows a victim's identity exhaust that budget and lock the victim out; a per-source\n        // budget alone would let a distributed attacker hammer one account. Because they are\n        // separate counters, exhausting one never consumes the other.\n        this.perAddressLimiter = new FixedWindowRateLimiter(\n                settings.maxIssuesPerIpPerMinute(), WINDOW_MILLIS, settings.maxTrackedRateLimitKeys());\n        this.perSubjectLimiter = new FixedWindowRateLimiter(\n                settings.maxIssuesPerSubjectPerMinute(), WINDOW_MILLIS, settings.maxTrackedRateLimitKeys());\n        this.fingerprint = new LogFingerprint();\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Validates the pending session and, if everything checks out, writes the session cookie\n     * and the accompanying response hardening headers.\n     *\n     * <p>This method never writes a status code or a body -- the servlet translates the\n     * returned {@link IssueOutcome} into one of a fixed set of generic responses. Keeping\n     * the two apart means an outcome can never be reported as success while the cookie was\n     * not actually written.</p>\n     */\n    public IssueOutcome issue(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(request, \"request\");\n        Objects.requireNonNull(response, \"response\");\n        long now = clock.millis();\n\n        if (response.isCommitted()) {\n            LOG.warn(\"Session cookie not issued: response already committed\");\n            return IssueOutcome.UNAVAILABLE;\n        }\n        if (settings.secure() && !request.isSecure()) {\n            // A Secure cookie sent over cleartext is dropped by the browser, and the\n            // identifier would have travelled in the clear to get there. Refuse instead.\n            LOG.warn(\"Session cookie not issued: Secure cookies configured but request is not over TLS\");\n            return IssueOutcome.UNAVAILABLE;\n        }\n        if (request.getAttribute(ISSUED_MARKER_ATTRIBUTE) != null) {\n            LOG.warn(\"Session cookie not issued: a cookie was already issued for this request\");\n            return IssueOutcome.UNAVAILABLE;\n        }\n\n        String clientAddress = addressResolver.resolve(request);\n        if (!perAddressLimiter.tryAcquire(\"a|\" + clientAddress, now)) {\n            LOG.warn(\"Session cookie issuance throttled for source {}\", fingerprint.of(clientAddress));\n            return IssueOutcome.RATE_LIMITED;\n        }\n\n        String sessionId = request.getAttribute(SESSION_ID_ATTRIBUTE) instanceof String s ? s : null;\n        boolean wellFormed = SessionIdValidator.isValid(sessionId);\n\n        Optional<TrustedSession> session;\n        try {\n            session = wellFormed ? lookup(sessionId) : Optional.empty();\n        } catch (RuntimeException e) {\n            // Log the type and stack trace, never the identifier.\n            LOG.error(\"Session lookup failed for session {}\", fingerprint.of(sessionId), e);\n            return IssueOutcome.UNAVAILABLE;\n        }\n\n        // The subject budget is charged on every attempt that got this far, including the\n        // ones that found nothing, using a source-derived placeholder key. Both branches do\n        // one fingerprint and one atomic map update, so the time taken and the state touched\n        // do not reveal whether the identifier was real.\n        String subjectKey = session.map(TrustedSession::subjectKey).orElse(\"anon|\" + clientAddress);\n        boolean withinSubjectBudget = perSubjectLimiter.tryAcquire(\"s|\" + fingerprint.of(subjectKey), now);\n\n        if (session.isEmpty() || !withinSubjectBudget) {\n            LOG.info(\"Session cookie refused for source {} (session {})\",\n                    fingerprint.of(clientAddress), fingerprint.of(sessionId));\n            return IssueOutcome.REJECTED;\n        }\n\n        long maxAge = effectiveMaxAge(session.get().remainingLifetimeSeconds());\n        String header = SetCookieHeaderFactory.issue(settings, sessionId, maxAge);\n\n        applyResponseHardening(response);\n        response.addHeader(\"Set-Cookie\", header);\n        request.setAttribute(ISSUED_MARKER_ATTRIBUTE, Boolean.TRUE);\n\n        LOG.info(\"Session cookie issued for subject {} (session {}, maxAge={}s)\",\n                fingerprint.of(session.get().subjectKey()), fingerprint.of(sessionId), maxAge);\n        return IssueOutcome.ISSUED;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Writes a header that deletes the session cookie, for use by the logout endpoint after\n     * the trusted component has invalidated the session server-side. Clearing the cookie is\n     * cosmetic on its own -- a copy already taken by an attacker is unaffected -- so it must\n     * never be the only step in a logout.\n     */\n    public void expireSessionCookie(HttpServletResponse response) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(response, \"response\");\n        if (response.isCommitted()) {\n            LOG.warn(\"Session cookie not cleared: response already committed\");\n            return;\n        }\n        applyResponseHardening(response);\n        response.addHeader(\"Set-Cookie\", SetCookieHeaderFactory.expire(settings));\n    }\n\n    private Optional<TrustedSession> lookup(String sessionId) {\n        Optional<TrustedSession> found = sessionService.lookupActiveSession(sessionId);\n        return found == null ? Optional.empty() : found;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * The cookie must never outlive the server-side session: a browser holding a cookie for\n     * a session the server has already dropped produces confusing failures and widens the\n     * window in which a stolen cookie looks useful.\n     */\n    private long effectiveMaxAge(long remainingLifetimeSeconds) {\n        if (settings.maxAgeSeconds() == 0) {\n            return 0; // browser-session cookie, discarded when the browser closes\n        }\n        return Math.min(settings.maxAgeSeconds(), remainingLifetimeSeconds);\n    }\n\n    private static void applyResponseHardening(HttpServletResponse response) {\n        // A Set-Cookie carrying a session identifier must never be stored by a shared cache\n        // and handed to the next user of that cache.\n        response.setHeader(\"Cache-Control\", \"no-store\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Pragma\", \"no-cache\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Expires\", \"0\");\n        response.addHeader(\"Vary\", \"Cookie\");\n        response.setHeader(\"X-Content-Type-Options\", \"nosniff\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Referrer-Policy\", \"same-origin\");\n    }\n\n    /** Releases the log-masking key. */\n    @Override\n    public void close() {\n        fingerprint.close();\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"1e0f15d4-e601-4a00-a2b7-895880b64c13","session_id":"68381359-87c5-42de-ba45-ad39766a06c0","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"1e0f15d4-e601-4a00-a2b7-895880b64c13","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Let this.maxAgeSeconds = b.maxAgeSeconds;\n        this.maxIssuesPerIpPerMinute = b.maxIssuesPerIpPerMinute;\n        this.maxIssuesPerSubjectPerMinute = b.maxIssuesPerSubjectPerMinute;\n The message names the\n     *                                  offending key but never echoes its value, so a value\n     *                                  mistakenly holding a secret cannot reach a {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Configuration value is empty: \" + key);\n        }\n        return trimmed;\n    }\n\n    private static String requireCookieName(String name) {\n i++) {\n            char c = name.charAt(i);\n            boolean ok = (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') || (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= '0' && c <= '9')\n                    || c == '-' || c == '_' || c == '.' || c == '~';\n            if (!ok) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Invalid SESSION_COOKIE_NAME: illegal character\");\n            }\n        }\n        return name;\n javax.crypto.spec.SecretKeySpec;\n\n/**\n * Turns a sensitive value into a short, non-reversible tag that is safe to log.\n *\n * <p>Session identifiers and subject keys must never appear in logs: log files outlive the\n * session, are shipped to aggregators, and are readable by operators who are not entitled\n * to impersonate users. A plain hash is not enough either -- the identifier space is\n * guessable enough that an attacker with log access could confirm a stolen identifier by\n * hashing it. com.example.websession;\n\n/**\n * Result of an attempt to hand the session cookie to the browser.\n *\n * <p>The enum is deliberately coarse. Internally the component distinguishes a missing\n * identifier from a malformed one from an unknown one, but all three collapse into\n * {@link #REJECTED} before anything leaves the process, so no caller -- and therefore no\n * response -- can tell them apart.</p>\n */\npublic enum IssueOutcome {\n\n    /** The cookie was written to the response. */\n    ISSUED,\n\n    /**\n     * No cookie. Covers a missing identifier, a malformed identifier, an identifier that is\n     * not live server-side, and a per-subject throttle. Always reported to the client as a\n     * single generic authentication failure.\n     */\n    REJECTED,\n\n    /** Too many issuance attempts from this source address. */\n    RATE_LIMITED,\n\n    /**\n     * The component refused to act: the response was already committed, the connection was\n     * not secure while Secure cookies are required, a cookie had already been issued for\n     * this request, or the session lookup failed. Let me look for subtle bugs.\n\nLet me carefully analyze the ClientAddressResolver X-Forwarded-For handling:\n\n```java\nString[] hops = header.split(\",\", -1);\nint index = hops.length - trustedProxyCount;\nif (index < 0 || index >= hops.length) {\n    return direct;\n}\nString candidate = sanitize(hops[index].trim());\n```\n\nThe intent: with N trusted proxies, the client sends `X-Forwarded-For: <client-supplied values>` and then each trusted proxy appends. So the original client IP would be at position `hops.length - trustedProxyCount`. Wait — if there are N trusted proxies, each appends the peer IP it saw. The LAST (rightmost) entry is written by the closest proxy. Actually the standard: XFF list is left-to-right, original client first. Each proxy appends the IP it received the connection from. So with N trusted proxies, the rightmost N entries were written by trusted proxies. The client-controlled entries are everything to the left of those. So `hops.length - trustedProxyCount` gives the boundary — but that's the LAST client-controlled entry, not the first trusted-written one.\n\nLet me think about this more concretely. Suppose there's 1 trusted proxy. The real client connects to the proxy. The client sends `X-Forwarded-For: 1.2.3.4` (forged). The proxy appends the actual peer IP (the attacker's real IP, say `9.9.9.9`). Let me re-derive: total hops after N appends. If the client sent K entries, hops.length = K + N. The entries at indices 0..K-1 are client-supplied. Indices K..K+N-1 are proxy-written. But let me do one more careful pass for an actual security bug I might've missed — the timing-equalization claim, and the subject-rate-limit DoS.\n\n**Subject-budget lockout DoS (SEC finding):** An attacker who possesses (or can influence the trusted component to hand them) a *live* session identifier belonging to a victim can repeatedly POST to this endpoint, each time charging the victim's subject bucket (maxIssuesPerSubjectPerMinute, default 20/min), causing the victim's legitimate session re-issuance to be refused with 401. But to have the victim's live session ID you already own the session → full compromise already. So the marginal DoS is negligible. Conversely, guessing IDs charges the attacker's own `anon|address` bucket (bounded by per-address limit) — so no lockout of arbitrary victims by guessing. So the DoS-via-guessing is actually correctly mitigated. This is genuinely well-designed. I'll credit it in Section 6.\n\nNow, is there a way to charge a *victim's* subject bucket WITHOUT a valid session ID? subjectKey is derived from `session.map(...)`. Guessing → session empty → placeholder. So no. Good. So the memory angle: attacker controls a whole XFF header (up to 1KiB) but only one hop is read per request → at most 1 new key per request → bounded by request rate. So no memory DoS either.\n\nHmm, so that angle collapses too. Actually a cleaner fix: parse with a real IP parser. But the whole point was \"no DNS lookup,\" and `InetAddress.getByName` DOES do DNS for hostnames — that's why they hand-rolled. Client connects to p1. Each proxy appends the IP of its inbound peer. p1's inbound peer = real client. p2's inbound peer = p1. ... pN's inbound peer = p(N-1). App's `getRemoteAddr()` = pN.\n\nXFF built by appending: after p1: [realClient]. after p2: [realClient, p1]. ... after pN: [realClient, p1, ..., p(N-1)]. So hops (as seen by app) = K_client + N entries where K_client is entries the client forged. Suppose client sends `XFF: A1, A2, ..., Ak` (forged). Then final list = [A1..Ak, realClient, p1, ..., p(N-1)]. Total hops.length = k + N. The realClient is at index k. trustedProxyCount = N. index = hops.length - N = k + N - N = k. hops[k] = realClient. CORRECT. \n\nAnd the entries at index > k (p1..p(N-1)) are trusted-proxy addresses we don't want. hops[k] is exactly the outermost proxy's written value = real client. So the selection is CORRECT — no off-by-one. And crucially the attacker cannot control hops[k] because only p1 writes it (p1 appends realClient as observed; p1 strips or preserves client's forged prefix but p1's own appended value is realClient). Wait — p1 appends its inbound peer which is the real client's IP regardless of forged content. So hops[k]=realClient is not attacker-controllable. And to shift the index so that an attacker-controlled entry lands at index k, the attacker would need to change hops.length, i.e., send a different number of forged entries. If attacker sends k' forged entries, final length = k'+N, index = k'+N-N = k', and hops[k'] = realClient (still, because p1 still appends realClient right after the k' forged entries). So no matter how many forged entries the client prepends, index lands on realClient. Robust. Confirmed correct. \n\nNow what if attacker sends MORE entries than... there's no way to make index land on a forged value because index always = (number of entries before the proxy-written block) = number of forged entries = k', and hops[k'] is the first proxy-written = realClient. \n\nBut the fallback: `if (index < 0 || index >= hops.length) return direct;`. index = hops.length - N. index < 0 iff hops.length < N (fewer than promised → fall back). index >= hops.length iff -N >= 0 iff N<=0, but N>=1 here. So index in range. Good. And the \"fewer hops than promised\" case (client strips XFF, header missing → header==null → return direct). Actually if client sends NO XFF, header=null → return direct (=getRemoteAddr = pN, the proxy's IP!). Hmm — when trustedProxyCount>0 and client sends no XFF header, the code returns `direct` = pN (the closest proxy's IP), NOT realClient. That means requests without XFF get keyed to the proxy IP → all such clients share the proxy's bucket. But normally the trusted proxy ALWAYS appends XFF (creating it if absent), so header wouldn't be null. If pN always appends, header is never null when trustedProxyCount>0. If it's null (proxy misconfigured), fall back to direct=proxyIP. Fail-safe-ish (over-throttle, not under). Not a vuln.\n\nBut here's a genuine spoof: what if the attacker sends exactly N forged entries such that... no, shown above it's robust.\n\nWhat if attacker sends `XFF` with FEWER than N total hops by stripping? If total hops < N → fallback to direct=proxyIP. So attacker can't get a self-chosen key; worst case they get grouped with proxyIP bucket (shared, over-throttled). Can't bypass. Good.\n\nAlright, confirmed: no XFF spoofing bug. The code is correct.\n\nFinal: I'll produce an honest report with a small set of low-severity/defensive findings and strong Section 6. Security: check-then-act race, memory exhaustion.\n\n3. `GenericResponse.java` — fixed generic responses. Let me think about each carefully.\n\n**Potential finding 1: FixedWindowRateLimiter `windows.size()` check-then-act race on the max-keys cap.**\n\n```java\nif (windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys && !windows.containsKey(key)) {\n    purgeExpired(nowMillis);\n    if (windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys && !windows.containsKey(key)) {\n        return false;\n    }\n}\nWindow updated = windows.compute(key, ...);\n```\n\nThis is a check-then-act: between the size check and the `compute`, other threads can also pass the check and each add a new key, so the map can exceed `maxTrackedKeys`. ConcurrentHashMap.size() is also O(n)/not atomic snapshot. But the impact is bounded: each concurrent thread over the limit is at most one per thread, so overshoot is bounded by thread count. The key space is still bounded-ish (bounded by maxTrackedKeys + concurrent threads, and each key is bounded length). Actually this is a real but minor issue. The claim in the docstring \"the key space is bounded... fails closed\" is slightly overstated but the practical bound still holds. This is a real concurrency finding, low severity. The comment at FixedWindowRateLimiter.java:56-57 says the per-key compute is atomic — that part is true; the cap check is not. Let me note it as a real but low-severity finding. CWE-362/CWE-367 (TOCTOU).\n\nActually, is it exploitable? An attacker who can vary keys wants to grow the map unboundedly. But the cap check rejects new keys once full (fails closed), and the only overshoot is the number of concurrent threads at the moment of saturation — bounded by request concurrency. So memory growth is bounded by maxTrackedKeys + N_threads, not unbounded. So it's not really a memory-exhaustion vulnerability. It's a minor deviation from the \"exactly maxTrackedKeys\" guarantee. I'll report as Low with CWE-367 but be honest about bounded impact. Actually let me reconsider whether to report at all — reporting low-severity real issues is fine and honest.\n\n**Potential finding 2: `ClientAddressResolver.isIpLiteral` accepts IPv4-looking strings that are actually hex — dotted-quad check is separate.** Let me look. For IPv6 detection: if sawColon, return value.length()>=2. So \"::1\" ok. But also \"deadbeef\" with no colon goes to isDottedQuad. Fine. Is there an issue where an attacker can supply a value that passes isIpLiteral but is not a real address and becomes a map key? Yes — e.g. \"1234567890:abcdef\" (colon, length>=2) passes. Or \"1.2.3.4.5\" no. Or \"....\" no (isDottedQuad fails). But since keys only feed the rate limiter and log HMAC, and there's no DNS lookup and it never goes into the header, this is benign. The only impact: attacker can create arbitrary distinct map keys → but keys are bounded by MAX_ADDRESS_LENGTH=45 and only used when trustedProxyCount>0 AND only the hop the proxy wrote is read. With trustedProxyCount>0, the value read is hops[len-count] which the trusted proxy wrote (the real client IP). An attacker controlling XFF cannot control that specific entry if the count is correct. So they can't mint arbitrary keys through XFF under correct config. If they send fewer hops, fall back to direct. If they send MORE hops, index = len-count points to an entry that could be attacker-supplied! Let me examine.\n\nSuppose trustedProxyCount=1. Header format normally: `client, proxy1`? Actually with one trusted proxy, the proxy appends the client IP it saw. So a legitimate request has `X-Forwarded-For: <client>`. hops = [<client>], len=1, index=1-1=0 → hops[0]=client. Good.\n\nBut a malicious client can pre-populate: send `X-Forwarded-For: 1.1.1.1, 2.2.2.2`. The trusted proxy appends the real client IP → header becomes `1.1.1.1, 2.2.2.2, <realclient>`. hops len=3, index=3-1=2 → hops[2]=realclient. Good — that's the rightmost, the proxy-written one. So attacker can't shift it. Wait, that's the point: `index = hops.length - trustedProxyCount` picks the entry written by the outermost trusted proxy counting from the right. With count=1 and 3 hops, index 2 = the last = proxy-written. Correct. So the algorithm correctly takes the rightmost trusted-written entry regardless of how many the attacker prepends. Good, that's actually the correct approach. So no spoofing there. \n\nBut wait — is it rightmost-count from the correct end? Standard XFF: leftmost is original client, each proxy appends to the right. The trusted proxies are the ones closest to the server. So server sees N trusted proxies each having appended. So the last N entries (rightmost N) are written by trusted proxies; specifically the (len - N)th index (0-based) is the entry written by the outermost trusted proxy, which is the first entry the trusted proxy chain added = the real client IP (as seen by outermost trusted proxy). index = hops.length - trustedProxyCount = len - N. That's exactly it. So for count=1, index=len-1 = rightmost = what the single trusted proxy appended. Correct. Good — the resolution is sound. Not a finding.\n\nActually let me double check with count=2. Two trusted proxies. Legit: client sends nothing (or attacker sends junk). Outermost trusted proxy appends realclient; innermost appends outermost-proxy's IP. So server receives `..., realclient, proxy1ip`. len-2 index → realclient. Correct. Good.\n\nSo ClientAddressResolver is correct. I won't report it as a vulnerability. It's a control that works. Good.\n\n**Potential finding 3: Timing side channel — `SessionIdValidator.isValid` short-circuits on length.** Not sensitive; identifier is server-generated. Let me reconsider the rate limiter's fail-open vs fail-closed and the `windows.size()` unbounded issue more carefully.** Actually already covered.\n\n**Potential finding 8: `FixedWindowRateLimiter` `purgeExpired` iterates the whole map (O(n)) under the `windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys` branch. With maxTrackedKeys up to 1,000,000, an attacker who fills the map then triggers the purge path on each request causes an O(1M) scan per request → CPU DoS (algorithmic complexity). This is a real availability concern: CWE-407 (Algorithmic Complexity). When the map is full and keys are not expired (attacker keeps refreshing distinct keys), every new request calls purgeExpired which scans all entries but removes nothing (all unexpired), then returns false. So each request does an O(maxTrackedKeys) iteration. With 1M keys and high request rate, that's serious CPU burn. But wait — to fill 1M keys, the attacker needs to actually add 1M distinct keys first, which requires 1M successful tryAcquire calls that each added a key (each also cheap, amortized). Once full, the attacker then hits with distinct new keys, and each triggers a full scan. But each such request also returns false (fails closed) after the scan. So the attacker pays: 1M requests to fill, then ongoing requests each cost a 1M-element iteration on the server. That's a legitimate amplification: attacker sends 1 request/second, server does 1M-iteration scan each. Actually the scan is on the request thread — real CPU amplification. This is a genuine CWE-407 / availability finding, medium-ish. But keys must be distinct — can the attacker produce distinct valid client addresses? Only if trustedProxyCount>0 and they can get distinct values into the trusted-proxy-written slot — they can't (shown above). Under trustedProxyCount=0, the address is getRemoteAddr() = real connection IP — attacker can't have 1M distinct IPs without a huge botnet, but a botnet is exactly the threat model for distributed rate limiting. Also getRemoteAddr is only attacker-varied via real source IPs. So filling 1M keys requires 1M distinct source IPs — hard. For the per-SUBJECT limiter, subjectKey is server-side, so an attacker can't easily mint 1M distinct subject keys (would need 1M live sessions). Hmm. So filling the map to maxTrackedKeys is hard for an attacker. So the purge-amplification is not easily attacker-triggerable. Lower confidence. I'll mention as Low, honestly caveated. Actually, given the difficulty, I may fold it into the concurrency/capacity note. Let me keep the report honest: the purge-on-full O(n) scan is bounded by maxTrackedKeys which is itself capped at 1M, and triggering requires filling the map which is hard. I'll report the purge cost as a Low finding under CWE-407 with clear caveats, or better, mention it in the concurrency finding. \n\nLet me reconsider — actually the more I think, this code is genuinely secure and the honest finding set is small. Correct.\n\nWhen maxAgeSeconds>0: emitMaxAge=true, and `if (maxAge==0) add Expires`. Since maxAge>0, no Expires added. Just Max-Age. Fine.\n\n**Reconsider the `windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys` fail-closed returning false (no permit)** — means when full, NEW keys are refused issuance (treated as rate-limited). For the per-address limiter that means new source IPs get 429 during exhaustion — fail closed, acceptable. For per-subject, new subject keys get charged false → withinSubjectBudget=false → REJECTED. Fail closed. OK.\n\nSo overall the genuinely reportable items are the two concurrency/complexity nuances in FixedWindowRateLimiter. Let me also reconsider whether the check-then-act size race is worth listing — I'll list it (CWE-367, Low) with honest bounded impact.\n\nActually, let me reconsider one more time for something more serious around **`ClientAddressResolver` when `trustedProxyCount > 0` and the header contains exactly enough hops but attacker controls the value at the selected index.** I proved index lands on the rightmost-trusted-written entry. But what if an attacker can send MORE entries than trustedProxyCount such that len - count still points to a real client slot, but the attacker ALSO can make len - count point to one of their own entries? index = len - count. Attacker controls the leftmost entries (their forgeries) and the real client IP is at some position. With count=1 and attacker sending K forged entries, after proxy appends, len = K+1, index = (K+1)-1 = K = last entry = the proxy-appended real client IP. So always the real IP. The attacker's forgeries are at indices 0..K-1, never index K. So safe. Confirmed. Good — genuinely not a finding; I'll note it as a verified control.\n\nLet me also verify there is no issue with `header.split(\",\", -1)` when count hops but a hop is empty (e.g., \"a,,b\"). sanitize(\"\")→UNKNOWN→candidate=UNKNOWN→return direct. Safe.\n\nAlright. Given the honest review, I'll report:\n- SEC-01: FixedWindowRateLimiter capacity cap is check-then-act (not atomic with the insert), so the map can transiently exceed maxTrackedKeys by up to the concurrency level; and the purge-on-full path is an O(maxTrackedKeys) scan per request once saturated (algorithmic amplification), CWE-367 (TOCTOU) / CWE-407. I'll combine? The format wants one CWE per finding ideally but allows one primary. Let me split into two findings to be precise:\n  - SEC-01 (Low, CWE-367): TOCTOU on the max-keys cap allows bounded overshoot.\n  - SEC-02 (Low, CWE-407): purgeExpired O(n) scan per saturated request = CPU amplification.\n  Both Low, honestly caveated (hard to trigger at scale; impact bounded).\n\nShould I report anything Medium? Honestly I don't find a Medium+ real vulnerability in this component within its stated scope. Fabricating one would be wrong. I'll clearly state that within the documented trust boundary the design is fail-closed and I found no high/critical issues, and list all verified controls in section 6.\n\nActually, let me reconsider SEC-02 severity/exploitability once more because I want the report to be defensible. To saturate the map with live (unexpired) keys, the attacker needs many distinct valid rate-limit keys within one 60s window. Per-address key = real source IP (or proxy-delegated real IP). A botnet with, say, 100k distinct IPs easily fills default maxTrackedKeys=100000. Then every further request from any new IP triggers a 100k-element purge scan that removes nothing (keys still fresh within the window as the botnet keeps cycling), returns false. At high request rates, each request costs 100k iterations → serious CPU amplification on the request path. And raising maxTrackedKeys (up to 1M) makes each scan worse. This IS plausibly reachable by the very distributed attacker the limiter is designed to resist. So CWE-407 is a legitimate Medium (amplification: cheap attacker request → expensive server work, on the critical path). I'll rate SEC-02 as Medium with this reasoning. That's honest and well-reasoned.\n\nSEC-01 (cap overshoot) — bounded by concurrency, Low.\n\nLet me also reconsider whether there's a finding that the **per-address and per-subject limiters share `maxTrackedRateLimitKeys` but are separate maps, so worst-case tracked keys = 2×**. Memory note only, not security. Skip.\n\nNow, is `windows.size()` on ConcurrentHashMap accurate? It returns a count that may be stale but is based on a counter maintained atomically (baseCount). It's effectively accurate modulo in-flight ops. The TOCTOU is real but overshoot bounded. Fine, Low.\n\nI think two findings (one Low, one Medium) is the honest outcome. Let me write the report. I must copy exact code excerpts and provide corrected code for each.\n\nFor SEC-02, the fix: bound purge work — either purge lazily/randomly-sampled (bounded number of entries) rather than full scan, or evict one expired entry via a bounded iterator with early exit, and if still full return false. Provide a corrected purgeExpired that stops after removing enough / scanning a bounded count. E.g., a bounded purge that iterates at most N entries.\n\nFor SEC-01 fix: perform the capacity decision inside compute (or use compute to atomically add only when below cap). Provide corrected tryAcquire that does the cap check inside compute and removes/declines atomically. But we can't easily return \"declined new key\" from compute without signaling; we can return null to not create the entry and detect null result. Let me craft: do purge once (bounded) before compute, then within compute, if existing==null and map is at/over cap, return null (don't insert) and treat as refused. But compute can't see updated size easily; however `windows.size()` inside compute is still approximate. Alternatively, accept that a fully atomic size cap requires a separate atomic counter. Simpler honest fix: maintain an AtomicInteger of tracked keys, and inside compute, when adding a new key, only increment/insert if counter < cap (CAS), else return null → declined. That makes the cap atomic and also avoids the pre-scan. I'll present a corrected version using an AtomicInteger and a bounded purge. This addresses both findings; but I should keep fixes scoped per finding. I'll give SEC-01 a compute-based atomic fix and SEC-02 a bounded-purge fix, noting they interrelate.\n\nLet me write concise corrected code.\n\nFor SEC-01, corrected tryAcquire (atomic cap via AtomicInteger + bounded purge reference):\n\n```java\nprivate final AtomicInteger tracked = new AtomicInteger();\n\nboolean tryAcquire(String key, long nowMillis) {\n    if (key == null) {\n        return false;\n    }\n    Window[] holder = new Window[1];\n    Window updated = windows.compute(key, (k, existing) -> {\n        boolean isNew = existing == null;\n        boolean expired = !isNew && nowMillis - existing.startMillis() >= windowMillis;\n        if (expired) {\n            return new Window(nowMillis, 1);\n        }\n        if (isNew) {\n            while (true) {\n                int cur = tracked.get();\n                if (cur >= maxTrackedKeys) {\n                    return null;            // at capacity and no expired slot reused -> refuse new key\n                }\n                if (tracked.compareAndSet(cur, cur + 1)) {\n                    break;\n                }\n            }\n            return new Window(nowMillis, 1);\n        }\n        int next = existing.count() == Integer.MAX_VALUE ? existing.count() : existing.count() + 1;\n        return new Window(existing.startMillis(), next);\n    });\n    if (updated == null) {\n        purgeExpired(nowMillis);           // opportunistic, bounded\n        return false;\n    }\n    return updated.count() <= permitsPerWindow;\n}\n```\n\nBut note when returning null from compute for a NEW key, compute leaves no mapping (good) and does not call remove for existing. But we also decrement tracked when an entry is removed in purgeExpired: decrement per removal. And expired-window roll (existing present but expired) reuses the key so no counter change. Good. This makes cap atomic. This is a fair amount of code but reasonable.\n\nHmm, but there's subtlety: returning `null` from compute when existing==null means no mapping is recorded — correct, key not added, tracked not incremented. attacks\n- Preventing API abuse and resource exhaustion from automated scripts and bots\n- Implementing fair usage quotas for different API consumer tiers (free, premium, enterprise)\n- Defending Standard API endpoints\n    \"api\": {\n        \"free\": {\"requests\": 60, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"premium\": {\"requests\": 300, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"enterprise\": {\"requests\": 1000, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n    },\n    # Resource-intensive endpoints\n    \"expensive\": {\n        \"search\": {\"requests\": 10, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"export\": {\"requests\": 5, \"window_seconds\": 3600, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"bulk_import\": {\"requests\": 2, \"window_seconds\": 3600, \"by\": \"user\"},\n    },\n    # Remove expired entries\n        pipe.zremrangebyscore(key, 0, window_start)\n        # Count requests in current window\n        pipe.zcard(key)\n        # Add current request\n        pipe.zadd(key, {f\"{now}:{hashlib.md5(str(now).encode()).hexdigest()[:8]}\": now})\n        # Set TTL on the key\n        pipe.expire(key, window_seconds + 1)\n\n        results = pipe.execute()\n        current_count = results[1]\n\n        if current_count >= max_requests:\n            # Calculate retry-after\n            oldest = self.redis.zrange(key, 0, 0, withscores=True)\n            if oldest:\n                retry_after = int(oldest[0][1] + window_seconds - now) + 1\n            else:\n                retry_after = window_seconds\n            return False, current_count, max_requests, retry_after\n\n        return True, current_count + 1, max_requests, 0\n\nrate_limiter = SlidingWindowRateLimiter(redis_client)\n\ndef rate_limit(max_requests, window_seconds, key_func=None):\n    \"\"\"Decorator for rate limiting API endpoints.\"\"\"\n    def decorator(f):"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"9f996db8-605f-484b-88b7-82e92c9a0a4e","session_id":"68381359-87c5-42de-ba45-ad39766a06c0","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"9f996db8-605f-484b-88b7-82e92c9a0a4e","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"I have enough. A plain hash is not enough either -- the identifier space is\n * guessable enough that an attacker with log access could confirm a stolen identifier by\n * hashing it. So the tag is an HMAC under a key generated at startup with\n * {@link SecureRandom} and never persisted or exported: correlation within one process\n * lifetime works, offline confirmation does not.</p>\n *\n * <p>The key is held in a {@code byte[]} so {@link #close()} can zero it; there is no\n * configuration file or environment variable that could leak it.</p>\n */\npublic final class LogFingerprint implements AutoCloseable {\n\n    private static final String ALGORITHM = \"HmacSHA256\";\n    private static final int TAG_HEX_CHARS = 12;\n    private static final char[] HEX = \"0123456789abcdef\".toCharArray();\n\n    private final byte[] key = new byte[32];\n    private volatile boolean closed;\n\n    public LogFingerprint() {\n        new SecureRandom().nextBytes(key);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * @return a short hex tag for {@code value}, or {@code \"-\"} for a null/blank value.\n     *         Never returns any part of the input.\n     */\n    public String of(String value) {\n        if (value == null || value.isEmpty() || closed) {\n            return \"-\";\n        }\n        byte[] input = value.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n        byte[] mac;\n        try {\n            Mac hmac = Mac.getInstance(ALGORITHM);\n            hmac.init(new SecretKeySpec(key, ALGORITHM));\n            mac = hmac.doFinal(input);\n        } catch (java.security.GeneralSecurityException e) {\n            // Never surface the input in a failure path.\n            return \"-\";\n        } finally {\n            Arrays.fill(input, (byte) 0);\n        }\n        StringBuilder out = new StringBuilder(TAG_HEX_CHARS);\n        for (int i = 0; out.length() < TAG_HEX_CHARS; i++) {\n            out.append(HEX[(mac[i] >> 4) & 0x0f]).append(HEX[mac[i] & 0x0f]);\n        }\n        Arrays.fill(mac, (byte) 0);\n        return out.toString();\n    }\n\n    /** Zeroes the key material. Let's think: `windows.size() >= maxTrackedKeys` gate. If size < max, all threads pass the gate and add keys. Concurrency N threads → up to N keys added beyond the cap in one interleaving. Then next arrivals see size>=max → purge → refuse. So the overshoot is bounded by concurrency, NOT unbounded. So heap exhaustion is *not* unbounded — the map can exceed the cap by roughly the number of concurrent in-flight requests. Hmm. That weakens SEC-01 considerably as a DoS.\n\nBut wait — purgeExpired iterates and removes expired entries. It's still a genuine CWE-367 defect (TOCTOU) that violates the stated bound — the honest classification: Low severity (bound breach proportional to concurrency; the fail-closed behaviour then locks out legitimate new clients even earlier). I'll present as Low-Medium: severity Low, but honestly described. Filling the table is cheap for the attacker (each distinct value = 1 request that is itself allowed since it's a new key). With trustedProxyCount=1 and a client sending XFF, the selected hop is hops[length-1] = the last entry = the value the *proxy* appended... wait. Standard: `X-Forwarded-For: client, proxy1, proxy2`; with trustedProxyCount=1 (one trusted proxy, e.g. an ALB), the server should read `hops[length-1]`... but careful: the ALB appends the peer address, so the last entry is written by the ALB and is trustworthy-ish. The code computes `index = hops.length - trustedProxyCount` = length-1 → the rightmost, i.e. the entry written by the nearest trusted proxy. That's the *correct* algorithm (rightmost counting from the trusted proxy). An attacker prepending junk entries shifts the array but the rightmost entry is still what the trusted proxy wrote... UNLESS the client itself sends `XFF: <junk>, <victim IP>`... no — with a single trusted proxy, the proxy *appends* the observed peer to whatever arrived. So the header is `junk..., peerIP` → hops = [junk..., peerIP], index = length-1 → peerIP. Correct! So the selected entry is NOT attacker controlled in the standard case. BUT: if the trusted proxy *replaces* rather than appends (some proxies strip incoming XFF), it's still fine. The risk arises when the attacker's junk makes `hops.length < trustedProxyCount` → falls back to direct (safe), or when the attacker sends a header containing a comma so that the appended entry shifts... The attacker controls the left entries; the code reads index = length - trustedProxyCount. With trustedProxyCount=2 (e.g. CDN + ALB), index = length-2 = the entry written by the CDN = the CDN's peer = attacker-supplied? No: CDN writes its peer address (the ALB's? or client's?). Ugh, in a 2-proxy chain, hops = [clientIP(as seen by CDN), proxy1IP?]... Actually each proxy appends the address of its *incoming peer*: CDN appends client IP; ALB appends CDN's egress IP. So hops = [clientIP, cdnIP]; index = length-2 = clientIP — the value the CDN wrote, which is the true client. Attacker-prepended junk shifts: attacker sends `XFF: a, b, c` → CDN appends real client → `a,b,c,clientIP`; ALB appends cdnIP → `a,b,c,clientIP,cdnIP`, length=5, index=5-2=3 → clientIP. Correct again! Because the attacker's entries are always to the left of everything, and rightmost-counting by trusted count lands exactly on the last trusted-written entry = real client. That's why this pattern is standard (and why the README/doc claim is right).\n\nThe bypass only exists if trustedProxyCount is set HIGHER than the real number of trusted proxies (overcount): then index moves left into attacker territory → attacker fully controls the selected value → both budget bypass and slot exhaustion. That's a *misconfiguration* hazard, and the code has a partial guard (index<0 → fallback) but cannot detect overcounting when the attacker pads the header with enough commas to keep index in range! That's the sharp finding: the code cannot distinguish \"chain is as promised\" from \"attacker padded the chain\", because `index = hops.length - trustedProxyCount` always succeeds once the attacker supplies >= trustedProxyCount entries. With trustedProxyCount=2 and attacker sending 2 junk hops, index = length-2 lands on... let's redo: real chain behind 2 proxies where attacker padded: `a,b,<realclient>,<cdnEgress>` — no wait, if there are exactly 2 trusted proxies, length after padding = 2 real + 2 junk = 4, index = 4-2 = 2 → `<realclient>`? Order: header arrives at CDN as `a,b`; CDN appends its peer (the real client IP as seen by CDN) → `a,b,realclient`; then ALB appends CDN's egress IP → `a,b,realclient,cdnEgress`; length=4, index=2 → realclient. Still correct!! Because rightmost-counting is robust to left-padding *provided exactly trustedProxyCount proxies each appended exactly one entry*. Left-padding increases both length and the number of left entries by the same amount, so index tracks correctly. So the algorithm is actually robust to attacker padding.\n\nThe failure mode is only undercount/overcount relative to reality. If operator sets trustedProxyCount=3 when only 2 append (e.g. one proxy strips XFF entirely and doesn't append — CloudFront doesn't append, it forwards + appends... some WAFs replace), then index=1 in `a,b,realclient,cdnEgress` (length 4, index 4-3=1) → 'b' = attacker value.
